\tilde{g}(t)
and the resulting solution to the normalized Ricci flow.

Remark 10.8
.

The main statement and its proof about the corresponding immortal flows reaching a hyperbolic orbifold from Theorem
1.4
are virtually the same as those of Theorem
10.4

by imposing
\Lambda=-3
and by flipping the sign of the time coordinate. Therefore we omit the proof of the curvature estimates stated in Theorem
1.4
.

10.2.1.
Curvature estimates on the perturbed bubble

On Eguchi-Hanson
(N^{4},\mathbf{eh})
, we can compute the exact norm of the obstructions in order to show that the Ricci curvature stays pinched.

Recall the metric

(10.1)

\mathbf{eh}:=\sqrt{\frac{r^{4}}{1+r^{4}}}(dr^{2}+r^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2})+\sqrt{1+r^{4}}(\alpha_{2}^{2}+\alpha_{3}^{2}),

and the infinitesimal Ricci-flat ALE deformations:

(1)

\mathbf{o}_{1}:=-\frac{r^{2}}{(1+r^{4})^{3/2}}(dr^{2}+r^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2})+\frac{1}{(1+r^{4})^{1/2}}(\alpha^{2}_{2}+\alpha_{3}^{2})
,

(2)

\mathbf{o}_{2}:=\frac{1}{1+r^{4}}(rdr\cdot\alpha_{2}-r^{2}\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{3})
,

(3)

\mathbf{o}_{3}:=\frac{1}{1+r^{4}}(rdr\cdot\alpha_{3}+r^{2}\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2})
.

Lemma 10.9
.

We have the following pointwise norms for
i=1,2,3
:

|\mathbf{o}_{i}|_{\mathbf{eh}}=\frac{2}{1+r_{b}^{4}}.

Proof.

Consider the orthonormal basis of
1
-forms
e^{0}:=\Big(\frac{r^{4}}{1+r^{4}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}dr
,
e^{1}:=\Big(\frac{r^{4}}{1+r^{4}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}r\alpha_{1}
,
e^{2}:=(1+r^{4})^{\frac{1}{4}}\alpha_{2}
and
e^{3}:=(1+r^{4})^{\frac{1}{4}}\alpha_{3}
for
\mathbf{eh}
. We then notice that

(1)

\mathbf{o}_{1}=\frac{1}{1+r^{4}}(-(e^{0})^{2}-(e^{1})^{2}+(e^{2})^{2}+(e^{3})^{2})
,

(2)

\mathbf{o}_{2}=\frac{1}{1+r^{4}}(e^{0}e^{2}+e^{2}e^{0}-e^{1}e^{3}-e^{3}e^{1})

(3)

\mathbf{o}_{3}=\frac{1}{1+r^{4}}(e^{0}e^{3}+e^{3}e^{0}+e^{1}e^{2}+e^{2}e^{2})
.

In particular, we see that

|\mathbf{o}_{i}|_{\mathbf{eh}}=\frac{2}{1+r^{4}},

as desired.
∎

We are in a good position to state and prove curvature estimates at the linear level on the perturbed bubble:

Proposition 10.10
.

If
\mathbf{R}^{+}_{p}=\pm\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{\Lambda^{+}}
then one has for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
:

(1)

d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}\mathring{\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits}(h_{2,\zeta})=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{o}_{i}(\zeta)=\mp\mathbf{o}_{1}(\zeta)
,

(2)

d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}\mathbf{R}^{+}(h_{2,\zeta})=\pm\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{\Lambda^{+}}
, and consequently

(3)

d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}W^{+}(h_{2,\zeta})=0
.

In particular, on
(N^{4},\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta})
,

\left|d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}{\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits}(h_{2,\zeta})-\Lambda\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}\right|_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}=\frac{2|\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{2}+r_{o}^{4}},

and the following pinching holds:

\left(\Lambda-\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{2}+r_{o}^{4}}\right)\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}\leq d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}{\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits}(h_{2,\zeta})\leq\left(\Lambda+\frac{|\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{2}+r_{o}^{4}}\right)\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}.

Proof.

Let us consider
h_{2}
an extension of
H_{2}
as in Proposition
2.13
. At the linear level, it induces the following curvature components on
\mathbf{eh}
, see
[
Biq16
]
.

•

d_{\mathbf{eh}}\mathring{\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits}(h_{2})=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{o}_{i}=-\sum_{i}R^{+}_{1i}\mathbf{o}_{i}
, where
R^{+}_{1i}
are the components of the matrix of the curvature
\mathbf{R}^{+}_{p}:\Lambda^{+}\to\Lambda^{+}
in the orthonormal basis
(\omega_{i}^{+})_{i}
of
\Lambda^{+}=\Lambda^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, i.e.
\mathbf{R}^{+}_{p}(\omega_{i}^{+})=\sum_{j}R_{ji}^{+}\omega_{j}^{+}=\sum_{j}R_{ij}^{+}\omega_{j}^{+}
.

•

d_{\mathbf{eh}}\mathbf{R}^{+}(h_{2})(\omega_{i}^{+})=\sum_{ij}R_{ij}^{+}\omega_{j}^{+}
.

This implies the three first expected properties since
\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}=|\zeta|(s_{\zeta})_{*}\mathbf{eh}
and
h_{2,\zeta}=|\zeta|^{2}(s_{\zeta})_{*}h_{2}
.

Moreover, Lemma
10.9
for
i=1
ensures that

\left|d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}{\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits}(h_{2,\zeta})-3\,\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}\right|_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}=|\mathbf{o}_{1}(\zeta)|_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}=\frac{2|\zeta|^{2}}{|\zeta|^{2}+r_{o}^{4}},

as desired since
\Lambda=3
.
Finally,
since
-\frac{1}{1+r_{b}^{4}}\mathbf{eh}\leq\mathbf{o}_{1}\leq\frac{1}{1+r_{b}^{4}}\mathbf{eh}
, we also have the corresponding pinching estimate on
d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2,\zeta})
thanks to item [(
1
), Proposition
10.10
] we just proved above.
∎

10.2.2.
Curvature estimates of the approximate solution

The goal of this section is to prove curvature estimates similar to those of Proposition
10.10
at a non-linear level for the approximate solution
(\tilde{g}(t))_{t\,\leq\,T}
. More precisely:

Proposition 10.11
.

Under the assumption and notations of Theorem
10.4
, there exists
T\leq 0
such that,

(1)

(Curvature tensor)
For
t\leq T
,

C_{1}|\zeta(t)|^{-1}\leq\sup_{M}|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))|_{\tilde{g}(t)}\leq C_{2}|\zeta(t)|^{-1},

for some positive uniform constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and there exists
C>0
such that for
t\leq T
,

\sup_{M}|\mathbf{R}^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))-\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{\Lambda^{+}}|_{\tilde{g}(t)}\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{2\delta}.

(2)

(Ricci curvature)
There exists
C>0
such that for
t\leq T
,

\sup_{M}|\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))|_{\tilde{g}(t)}\leq C.

For
\theta>0
sufficiently small with respect to the supremum of the diameters of
(M,\tilde{g}(t))_{t\,\leq\,T}
, there exists
C(\theta)>0
such that for
t\leq T
:

\sup_{\{r_{o}\,\geq\,\theta\delta(t)\}}|\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)|_{\tilde{g}(t)}\leq C(\theta)\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{4-6\delta}.

(3)

(Scalar curvature) There exists
C>0
such that for
t\leq T
,

\sup_{M}|\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits_{\tilde{g}(t)}-4\Lambda|\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{4-6\delta}.

(4)

(Global Ricci pinching)
There exists
C>0
such that for
t\leq T
,

\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))\geq\left(\Lambda-1-C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{4-6\delta}\right)\tilde{g}(t).

(5)

(Almost selfduality of
\tilde{g}(t)
)
For
t\leq T
,

\sup_{M}|W^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))|_{\tilde{g}(t)}\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{4-6\delta},

for some uniform positive constant
C
.

Proof.

The upper bound on the curvature tensor of
\tilde{g}(t)
is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
3.4
. The lower bound is obtained by estimating the curvature on the bubble region
\{r_{o}\,\leq\,\varepsilon(t)\}
from below the same way we did for the upper bound in the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
Therefore, for
t\leq T
,

C_{1}|\zeta(t)|^{-1}\leq\sup_{M}|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))|_{\tilde{g}(t)}\leq C_{2}|\zeta(t)|^{-1},

for some positive time-independent constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.

Now,
\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)=0
on
\{r_{o}>2\delta(t)\}
by construction of
\tilde{g}(t)
and choice of
g_{o}
. On
\{r_{o}<\delta(t)/2\}
,

(10.2)

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)&=\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}+h_{2,\zeta(t)})-\Lambda(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}+h_{2,\zeta(t)})\\
&=d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2,\zeta(t)})-\Lambda\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{B_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}(h_{2,\zeta(t)})}\tilde{g}(t)\\
&\quad+Q_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}(h_{2,\zeta(t)})-\Lambda h_{2,\zeta(t)}\\
&=d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2,\zeta(t)})-\Lambda\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}+Q_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}(h_{2,\zeta(t)})-\Lambda h_{2,\zeta(t)},\end{split}

where we have used that
h_{2,\zeta(t)}
is in Bianchi gauge by Proposition
2.13
.

Proposition
10.10
then gives on
\{r_{o}\geq\theta\delta(t)\}
,

\begin{split}|\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)|_{\tilde{g}(t)}&\leq C(\varepsilon(t)^{4}\theta^{-4}\delta(t)^{-4}+\delta(t)^{2})\leq C(\theta)\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\min\{4(1-\delta),\,2\delta\}}\\
&\leq C(\theta)\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{2\delta}\end{split}

as desired since
\delta<2/3
by (

H
0

). Here Proposition
2.13
is used again to handle the terms
Q_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}(h_{2,\zeta(t)})
and
h_{2,\zeta(t)}
.

As for the global pinching, it follows from (
10.2
) and from Proposition
10.10
that:

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)&\geq-\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}-C\delta(t)^{2}\tilde{g}(t)\\
&\geq-\left(1+C\delta(t)^{2}\right)\tilde{g}(t),\end{split}

as expected on
\{r_{o}<\delta(t)/2\}
. Here we have invoked Lemma
3.4
in the last line. In order to globalize the above pinching estimate, observe first that it holds trivially on
\{r_{o}>2\delta(t)\}
. Next, by linearizing
\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)
over
{\{r_{o}<3\delta(t)/2\}}
(respectively on
{\{r_{o}>\delta(t)\}}
) with respect to
\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}
(respectively to
g_{o}
), the same estimate holds thanks to Lemma
3.4
up to an error of magnitude
\varepsilon(t)^{4}\delta(t)^{-6}
. The boundedness of the Ricci tensor can be proved along a similar reasoning.

Regarding the behavior of the scalar curvature
\mathop{\rm R}\nolimits_{\tilde{g}(t)}
, it suffices to look at that of
\mathbf{R}^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))
since
M
has dimension
4
. Proposition
10.10
gives on
\{r_{o}<\delta(t)/2\}
:

\mathbf{R}^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))=d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}}\mathbf{R}^{+}(h_{2,\zeta})+O(\delta(t)^{2})=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{\Lambda^{+}}+O(\delta(t)^{2}).

On
\{r_{o}>2\delta(t)\}
,
\mathbf{R}^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))=\mathbf{R}^{+}(g_{o})=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{\Lambda^{+}}
by assumption on
g_{o}
. By linearizing
\mathbf{R}^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))
over
{\{r_{o}<3\delta(t)/2\}}
(respectively on
{\{r_{o}>\delta(t)\}}
) with respect to
\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}
(respectively to
g_{o}
), the same estimate holds thanks to Lemma
3.4
again up to an error of magnitude
\varepsilon(t)^{4}\delta(t)^{-6}
.

As for the decay of
W^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))
, notice that thanks to Proposition
10.10
, the following estimate holds on
{\{r_{o}<3\delta(t)/2\}}
:

\begin{split}W^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))&=W^{+}(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}+h_{2,\zeta(t)})+O(\varepsilon(t)^{4}\delta(t)^{-6})\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}d^{2}_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)+sh_{2,\zeta(t)}}}W^{+}(h_{2,\zeta(t)},h_{2,\zeta(t)})(1-s)\,ds+O(\varepsilon(t)^{4}\delta(t)^{-6})\\
&=O(\delta(t)^{2})+O(\varepsilon(t)^{4}\delta(t)^{-6})=O(\varepsilon(t)^{4}\delta(t)^{-6}),\end{split}

since
\delta>1/2
thanks to (

H
0

).
Here we have used Lemma
C.1
together with Lemma
3.4
in the first line together with a Taylor expansion in the second line. Observe the necessity of the property
d_{\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta(t)}}W^{+}(h_{2,\zeta(t)})=0
coming from
W^{+}_{p}(g_{o})=0
here.
A similar result holds when linearizing on
{\{r_{o}>\delta(t)\}}
with respect to
g_{o}
since
W^{+}(g_{o})=0
.
∎

10.2.3.
Curvature estimates of the solution

We first recall and prove the following lemma that gives a well-known one-to-one correspondance between a Ricci flow and a Ricci flow with extra linear terms and a Lie derivative term,

Lemma 10.12
.

For
T\leq 0
and
\beta\in L^{1}(-\infty,T)
, let
(g(t))_{t\,\leq\,T}
solve

\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g(t))+(\beta(t)+2\Lambda)g(t)+\mathcal{L}_{V(t)}g(t),\quad\text{on $M\times(-\infty,T)$},

for some
C^{1}
vector field
V(t)
. Let
(\varphi_{t})_{t\,\leq\,T}
be the flow of
-V(t)
.
Then the metrics
\bar{g}(\bar{t}):=c(t)\varphi_{t}^{*}g(t)
satisfies

\partial_{\bar{t}}\bar{g}(\bar{t})=-2\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))+2\Lambda\bar{g}(\bar{t}),\quad\text{on $M\times(-\infty,0)$},

where
c(t):=\exp\left(-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\beta(s)\,ds\right)
and
\bar{t}(t):=-\int_{t}^{T}c(s)\,ds.

Proof.

It is a straightforward computation: define
\bar{g}(\bar{t}):=c(t)\varphi_{t}^{*}g(t)
for some smooth positive function
c(\cdot)
and a smooth function
\bar{t}
to be defined later. The family of metrics
\bar{g}(\bar{t})
satisfies:

\begin{split}\bar{t}^{\prime}\partial_{\bar{t}}\bar{g}(\bar{t})&=\left(c^{\prime}(t)+c(t)(\beta(t)+2\Lambda)\right)\varphi_{t}^{*}g(t)-2c(t)\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\varphi_{t}^{*}g(t))\\
&=\left(c^{\prime}(t)+c(t)(\beta(t)+2\Lambda)\right)\varphi_{t}^{*}g(t)-2c(t)\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))\\
&=(c(t)\left(-2\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))+2\Lambda\bar{g}(\bar{t})\right),\end{split}

provided
\bar{t}^{\prime}(t)=c(t)
and
c^{\prime}(t)+c(t)\beta(t)=0
for all
t\in(-\infty,T)
. Here we have used the invariance of the Ricci curvature under scalings and diffeomorphisms of the metric in the second equality.

Therefore, if we choose
c(t):=\exp\left(-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\beta(s)\,ds\right)
and
\bar{t}(t)
to be
-\int_{t}^{T}c(s)\,ds
, we reach the desired conclusion.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
10.4
:

Proof of Theorem
10.4
.

Observe that since
g(t)
satisfies

\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g(t))+(2\Lambda+\beta(t))g(t)+\mathcal{L}_{V(t)}(g(t)),

where
V(t)
is defined as in Proposition
8.10
,
the solution to the normalized Ricci flow
\bar{g}(\bar{t})
associated to
g(t)
through Lemma
10.12
solving
\partial_{\bar{t}}\bar{g}(\bar{t})=-2\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))+2\Lambda\bar{g}(\bar{t})
only differs from
g(t)
by rescaling in space (by a factor
\exp\left(-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\beta(s)\,ds\right)=1+O(\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{2})
), time-reparametrization and diffeomorphisms of
M
. In particular, it suffices to prove the desired curvature estimates for
g(t)
only.

Now, linearizing the curvature operator of
g(t)
around
\tilde{g}(t)
via Lemma
C.1
gives:

\begin{split}|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(g(t))-\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))|_{g(t)}&\leq C\left(|\nabla^{\tilde{g}(t),2}k(t)|_{g(t)}+|\nabla^{\tilde{g}(t)}k(t)|^{2}_{g(t)}+|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))|_{g(t)}|k(t)|^{2}_{g(t)}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-\sigma}+\gamma(t)^{-2}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-2\sigma}\right)\\
&\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\iota\sigma},\end{split}

so that the triangular inequality leads to the expected estimate through Proposition
10.11
.

Similarly, linearizing the Ricci curvature of
g(t)
around
\tilde{g}(t)
gives:

(10.3)

\begin{split}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g(t))-\Lambda g(t)&=\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{L,\tilde{g}(t)}k(t)+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\rm\mathscr{L}}\nolimits_{B_{\tilde{g}(t)}(k(t))}\tilde{g}(t)+Q_{\tilde{g}(t)}(k(t))-\Lambda k(t)\\
&=\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)+O(\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-\sigma})+O(\gamma(t)^{-2}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-2\sigma})\\
&=\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))-\Lambda\tilde{g}(t)+O(\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\iota\sigma}),\end{split}

which gives the expected result by invoking Proposition
10.11
and (

H
1

) to ensure that
4-6\delta>\iota\sigma
. Here we have used the fact that
|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\tilde{g}(t))|_{\tilde{g}(t)}=O(\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2})
ensured by Lemma
3.4
. As for the pinching of the Ricci curvature, we use (
10.3
) together with Proposition
10.11
to get the expected result.

Now, linearizing the component
\mathbf{R}^{+}(g(t))
of the curvature tensor of
g(t)
around
\tilde{g}(t)
gives thanks to Lemma
C.1
:

|\mathbf{R}^{+}(g(t))-\mathbf{R}^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))|_{g(t)}\leq C\left(\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-\sigma}+\gamma(t)^{-2}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-2\sigma}\right)\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\iota\sigma},

so that the triangular inequality together with Proposition
10.11
and (

H
1

) to ensure that
4-6\delta>\iota\sigma
lead to the desired decay.

Finally, linearizing the selfdual component of the Weyl tensor of
g(t)
around
\tilde{g}(t)
gives thanks to Lemma
C.1
:

|W^{+}(g(t))-W^{+}(\tilde{g}(t))|_{g(t)}\leq C\left(\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-\sigma}+\gamma(t)^{-2}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2-2\sigma}\right)\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\iota\sigma},

so that the triangular inequality together with Proposition
10.11
and (

H
1

) to ensure that
4-6\delta>\iota\sigma
lead to the desired decay. Indeed, the constraints from (

H
1

)
(6-\sigma)\delta<4-\sigma
and
\iota<1/3
imply
4-6\delta>\iota\sigma
.

Regarding the covariant derivatives of
\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(g(t))
, it suffices to invoke Shi’s estimates for a solution
(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))_{\bar{t}\,\leq\,0}
to the Ricci flow that we now recall: if
k\geq 0
, there exists
C_{k}>0
such that if
\bar{T}<0
,

(10.4)

\sup_{M}|\nabla^{\bar{g}(\bar{T}),\,k}\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{T}))|_{\bar{g}(\bar{T})}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{(-\bar{T})^{\frac{k}{2}}}\sup_{M\times[2\bar{T},\bar{T}]}|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))|_{\bar{g}(\bar{t})}.

Now, by adapting the proof of Lemma
10.12
,
\bar{g}(\bar{t})=c(t)\varphi_{t}^{*}g(t)
with
c^{\prime}(t)+(2\Lambda+\beta(t))c(t)=0
and
\bar{t}^{\prime}(t)=c(t)
for
t\leq T
and
\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=-V(t)\circ\varphi_{t}
, so that (
10.4
) gives for
\bar{t}(t)=\bar{T}
:

\begin{split}\sup_{M}|\nabla^{g(t),\,k}\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(g(t))|_{g(t)}&\leq\frac{C_{k}c(t)^{\frac{k}{2}+1}}{(\int_{t}^{T}c(s)\,ds)^{\frac{k}{2}}}\sup_{M\times[\bar{t}^{-1}(2\bar{T}),\,\bar{t}^{-1}(\bar{T})]}c(s)^{-1}|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(g(s))|_{g(s)}\\
&\leq C_{k}\sup_{M\times[\bar{t}^{-1}(2\bar{T}),\,\bar{t}^{-1}(\bar{T})]}|\mathop{\rm Rm}\nolimits(g(s))|_{g(s)}\leq C_{k}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-2},\end{split}

since
\varepsilon_{0}
is decreasing in time. Here we have used the curvature estimate proved above. Notice that implicit here is the consequence from Shi’s estimates that
g(t)
hence
k(t)
is smooth in space.

Finally, by standard interpolation inequalities, on
M_{o}\setminus\{r_{o}\leq r\}
for a fixed
r>0
and any
a>0
small enough with respect to the parameters
\delta
and
\iota\sigma
, there exists
T<0
such that for
t\leq T
,

(10.5)

|\nabla^{g(t),\,k}(\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g(t))-2\Lambda g(t))|_{g(t)}\leq C_{k}(a,r)\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\iota\sigma-a},

for some positive constant
C_{k}(a,r)
. In particular, the same holds true for the solution to the normalized Ricci flow associated to
g(t)
. Indeed since here the estimate holds away from the singular set we only need to observe that the diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{t}
generated by
-V(t)
preserve uniformly in time any neighborhood of a point that lies outside the singular set of
M_{o}
. To convince ourselves of this fact, let us compute the time derivative of
\varphi_{t}^{*}r_{o}
:

(10.6)

\begin{split}|\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}^{*}r_{o}|\leq|V(t)|_{g(t)}\circ\varphi_{t}&\leq C\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-1-\sigma}+C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{2}\varphi_{t}^{*}r_{o}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{2}\varphi_{t}^{*}r_{o}+C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{1+\iota\sigma},\end{split}

where we have used the fact that
\nabla^{\tilde{g}(t)}\tilde{v}(t)=O(\rho(t))
uniformly in time by Lemma
3.11
. Grönwall inequality applied to (
10.6
) implies the desired result if we impose
\varphi_{t}\big|_{t=T}=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{M}
.

Now that we know that (
10.5
) holds for the associated solution to the normalized Ricci flow, integration in time gives smooth local convergence of this solution to the orbifold metric
g_{o}
away from the singularities at an exponential rate.

We are left with proving the global convergence of the associated solution to the normalized Ricci flow to
g_{o}
in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology: by Lemma
10.12
, the associated normalized Ricci flow differs from
g(t)
by diffeomorphisms, scaling (by a factor converging to
1
as fast as
\int^{t}_{-\infty}\beta(s)\,ds=O(\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{2})
and a time-reparametrization so that it suffices to prove that
g(t)
converges to
g_{o}
in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology as
t
goes to
-\infty
. Now, since

(1-C\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-\sigma})\tilde{g}(t)\leq g(t)\leq(1+C\gamma(t)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{-\sigma})\tilde{g}(t),\quad t\leq T,

we know that
d_{GH}((M,d_{\tilde{g}(t)}),(M,d_{g(t)}))\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{1+\iota\sigma/2}
for
t\leq T
. In particular, it suffices to show that
\tilde{g}(t)
converges to
g_{o}
in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology: since the diameter of the bubbles are
O(\delta(t))
, this immediately implies the expected result, i.e.

\begin{split}d_{GH}((M,d_{g_{o}}),(M,d_{g(t)}))&\leq d_{GH}((M,d_{g_{o}}),(M,d_{\tilde{g}(t)}))+d_{GH}((M,d_{\tilde{g}(t)}),(M,d_{g(t)}))\\
&\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\delta}+C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{1+\iota\sigma/2}\leq C\varepsilon_{0}(t)^{\delta}.\end{split}

Here we use the map
\Pi_{t}:M\rightarrow M_{o}
defined for each
t\leq T
such that if
x\in\{r_{o}>2\delta(t)\}
,
\Pi_{t}(x)=x
and otherwise,
\Pi_{t}(x)=o
the singular point of
M_{o}
as an
C\delta(t)
-isometry.
∎

10.3.
Scales along the non renormalized Ricci flow

Let us finally determine the various scales along the original Ricci flow, i.e. along the unnormalized Ricci flow. Thanks to Lemma
10.12
, we see that to go from
\bar{g}(\bar{t})
satisfying
\partial_{\bar{t}}\bar{g}(\bar{t})=-2(\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\bar{g}(\bar{t}))-\Lambda\bar{g}(\bar{t}))
for
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
to a flow
\hat{g}(\hat{t})
satisfying
\partial_{\hat{t}}\hat{g}(\hat{t}\,)=-2\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\hat{g}(\hat{t}\,))
, we may perform the following changes of variables:

\hat{t}:=-\frac{e^{-2\Lambda\bar{t}}}{2\Lambda},\quad\text{ and }\quad\hat{g}(\hat{t}\,):=e^{-2\Lambda\bar{t}}\,\,\bar{g}(\bar{t}\,)=\big(-2\Lambda\hat{t}\big)\,\,\bar{g}_{-\frac{\log(-2\Lambda\hat{t}\,)}{2\Lambda}}.

Now, as expected, this tells us that the resulting metric in the orbifold regions
r_{o}>\delta_{0}>0
evolves at leading order like an honest Einstein metric:

\hat{g}(\hat{t}\,)=\big(-2\Lambda\hat{t}\,\big)g_{o}

as
-2\Lambda\hat{t}
tends to
+\infty
. Similarly, one may look at the size of the Eguchi-Hanson metric (or any other Ricci-flat ALE metric) through Kronheimer’s period map. At leading order, we have
\bar{\zeta}(\bar{t}\,)=e^{2\bar{t}\mathbf{R}^{+}}\zeta_{0}
, which in the “hat” variables becomes

\hat{\zeta}(\hat{t}\,)=(-2\Lambda\hat{t}\,)\times\exp\Big(\log(-2\Lambda\hat{t}\,){\frac{2\mathbf{R}^{+}}{-2\Lambda}}\Big)\zeta_{0}.

Example 10.13
.

Let us detail the various scales on the main flows we construct:

•

For the unit round sphere, with
\Lambda=3
and
\mathbf{R}^{+}=\operatorname{I}_{3}
, we find that the orbifold metric evolves by
\hat{g}(\hat{t}\,)=(-6\hat{t}\,)g_{\mathbb{S}^{4}}
while the ALE region evolves by

\hat{\zeta}(\hat{t}\,)=(-6\hat{t}\,)^{1-\frac{1}{3}}\zeta_{0}=(-6\hat{t}\,)^{\frac{2}{3}}\zeta_{0}.

Along the actual Ricci flow, the curvature is therefore decaying as well as
\hat{t}\to-\infty
: it is in
(-6\hat{t}\,)^{-\frac{2}{3}}
while the curvature on the orbifold is in
(-6\hat{t}\,)^{-1}
.

•

For hyperbolic and complex hyperbolic orbifolds (in the orientation opposite to the Kähler one), with
\Lambda=-3
and
\mathbf{R}^{+}=-\operatorname{I}_{3}
, we find that the orbifold metric evolves by
\hat{g}(\hat{t}\,)=(6\hat{t}\,)g_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}
while the ALE region evolves by

\hat{\zeta}(\hat{t}\,)=(6\hat{t}\,)^{\frac{2}{3}}\zeta_{0}.

Along the actual Ricci flow, the curvature is therefore decaying like
(6\hat{t}\,)^{-\frac{2}{3}}
as
\hat{t}\to+\infty
.

As computed in
[
Ozu21
]
, a higher order approximation of the flat orbifold
\mathbb{T}^{4}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in the configuration considered in
[
BK17
]
with
\zeta(t)
parallel to
(1,0,0)
is interpreted as the orbifold behaving like a non-flat space at higher order, and satisfying

\mathbf{R}^{\pm}_{\zeta(t)}=c|\zeta(t)|^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{bmatrix}

at its singular points for some positive constant
c
. The positivity of
c|\zeta(t)|^{2}\times 2
in the first coefficient of the matrix explains the
growth
of the bubbles along the ancient flow of Brendle and Kapouleas, just like positive eigenvalues of
\mathbf{R}^{+}_{p}(g_{o})
in the present article. In terms of producing an approximate Ricci flow as we did along this article, if
\zeta(t)
stays in the direction
(1,0,0)
, then the ODE is

(10.7)

\dot{\zeta}(t)=\mathbf{R}^{\pm}_{\zeta(t)}\zeta(t)=2c|\zeta|^{2}\zeta,

and in particular
\varepsilon^{\prime}(t)=c\varepsilon(t)^{5}
using
\varepsilon(t)=|\zeta|^{\frac{1}{2}}
. This matches the ODE obtained in
[
BK17
]
, where the condition
\zeta(t)
in the direction
(1,0,0)
is ensured by imposing a set of discrete symmetries at every step of the construction and along the Ricci flow. Note that it is crucial for their construction, to use these discrete symmetries to avoid negative eigenvalues
-c|\zeta(t)|^{2}
of the selfdual curvature. This is also present in our work since we need to assume that
\mathbf{R}^{+}_{p}
has eigenvalues of the same sign to run our arguments.

11.
General Ricci-flat ALE metrics

In this section, we mainly extend the results of the previous sections to other hyperkähler Ricci-flat ALE metrics. We then justify the instability of spherical singularities, and the stability of hyperbolic singularities for potential non Kähler Ricci-flat ALE metrics in Section
11.5
.

11.1.
Kronheimer’s period map

As a transition with the next section, the hyperkähler 2-forms on the Eguchi-Hanson metric which are asymptotic to the
2
-forms
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})=dr\wedge r\alpha_{i}+r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}
for
(i,j,k)
cyclic permutation of
(1,2,3)
are:

\begin{split}\omega^{+}_{1}(\mathbf{eh})=\frac{r^{3}}{\sqrt{1+r^{4}}}dr\wedge\alpha_{1}&+\sqrt{1+r^{4}}\alpha_{2}\wedge\alpha_{3},\quad\omega^{+}_{2}(\mathbf{eh})=rdr\wedge\alpha_{2}+r^{2}\alpha_{3}\wedge\alpha_{1},\\
&\omega^{+}_{3}(\mathbf{eh})=rdr\wedge\alpha_{3}+r^{2}\alpha_{1}\wedge\alpha_{2},\end{split}

which are asymptotic to the
2
-forms
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})
.

The key point of the period map description of the moduli space of hyperkähler ALE metrics is that denoting
\Sigma
the
2
-sphere added at
\{r=0\}
, one has the following formula:

\int_{\Sigma}\omega_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{eh})=\operatorname{vol}(\mathbb{S}^{2})=4\pi\quad\text{ and for }i\in\{2,3\},\quad\int_{\Sigma}\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{eh})=0.

Now, if the Eguchi-Hanson metric is rotated as described in (
2.12
) for
\zeta=(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then, the
2
-forms
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta})
asymptotic to the
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})
on
\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}
satisfy:

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\omega_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta})=4\pi x,\quad\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\omega_{2}^{+}(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta})=4\pi y,\quad\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\omega_{3}^{+}(\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta})=4\pi z.

Up to a factor
4\pi
(that simplifies the computations in the case of an Eguchi-Hanson metric), in the rest of the section, we extend this parametrization to all of the known examples of Ricci-flat ALE spaces, and compute their evolutions along Ricci flow in what follows.

In general, according to Kronheimer’s period map
[
Kro89b
]
, hyperkähler ALE metrics may essentially be parametrized by
k
elements
\zeta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
i\in\{1,...,k\}
for some
k\geq 1
depending on the group at infinity. In the rest of the section, we will compute the “leading” terms in
r^{-4}
of the elements of
\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
. This will exhibit the link between the orthogonality of the leading terms of the elements of
\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
at infinity and their
L^{2}
-orthogonality.

This study is the key to explicitly understand the obstructions to gluing-perturbing to Einstein metrics and therefore understand the dynamics of Ricci flow close to an orbifold with arbitrary singularities with group in
\operatorname{SU}(2)
.

For a codimension
3
set of parameters
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3k}
, the associated metrics are singular and have additional orbifold singularities. This set is a cone over a codimension
3
set in
\mathbb{S}^{3k-1}
by homogeneity of the construction. We will always avoid this “forbidden” set
\mathcal{D}
for simplicity in this article.

Remark 11.1
.

Our construction should extend to trees of Ricci-flat ALE metrics. However, for Kähler Ricci-flat ALE metrics, this should not lead to new behaviors. Indeed, our analysis applies to flows starting arbitrarily close to the forbidden set
\mathcal{D}
. In other words, instead of developing trees of singularities at different scales, Ricci flow instead flows the whole tree of singularities at the same speed in our examples.

11.2.
Stability of hyperkähler ALE metrics

To lighten the notations, we denote
\omega_{i}^{+}=\omega_{i}^{+}(g_{b})
the hyperkähler forms of a given hyperkähler metric
g_{b}
.

The following result is well-known, but we add a short proof for completeness that will be helpful in the following sections.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let
g_{b}
be a hyperkähler ALE metric. Then, for any
h\in L^{2}
, one has
\langle\Delta_{L,g_{b}}h,h\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq 0
with equality if and only if
h\in\ker_{L^{2}}\Delta_{L,g_{b}}
, where

\ker_{L^{2}}\Delta_{L,g_{b}}=\operatorname{Span}\Big\{\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+},\quad\Omega_{j}^{-}\in\mathcal{H}^{-}_{L^{2}}(g_{b}),\quad i\in\{1,2,3\}\Big\},

where
\mathcal{H}_{L^{2}}^{-}(g_{b})
is the space of harmonic, anti-selfdual
2
-forms in
L^{2}(g_{b})
.

Proof.

It is well known that the Lichnerowicz Laplacian preserves pure-trace and traceless part on Einstein metrics. Moreover, for any smooth function
f
, one has

\Delta_{L,g_{b}}(f\cdot g_{b})=(\Delta_{g_{b}}f)\cdot g_{b},

so it is enough to prove the result for a traceless
2
-tensor
h
.

On traceless
2
-tensors, we may use the identification (
2.4
) to see that any traceless
2
-tensor
h
is equal to
h=\sum_{i}\phi_{i}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
for
\phi_{i}^{-}
anti-selfdual
2
-forms, and
\omega_{i}^{+}
a basis of Kähler forms of
g_{b}
. It is known from
[
BR09
, Formula (4.6)]
that in this decomposition, when the integration by parts is allowed (e.g. on a sequence of smooth compactly supported
2
-tensors approaching a given tensor in
L^{2}
), one has

\langle\Delta_{L,g_{b}}h,h\rangle_{L^{2}}=-4\sum_{i}\langle dd^{*}\phi_{i}^{-},\phi_{i}^{-}\rangle_{L^{2}}=-4\sum_{i}\|d^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

This proves the inequality
\langle\Delta_{L,g_{b}}h,h\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq 0
, and we moreover have equality if and only if the
\phi_{i}^{-}
are all
harmonic
, in which case
\Delta_{L,g_{b}}h=0
.
∎

Proposition 11.3
.

The elements of
\ker_{L^{2}}\Delta_{L,g_{b}}
decay as fast as
(1+r)^{-4}
at infinity.

Proof.

Let
h\in\ker_{L^{2}}\Delta_{L,g_{b}}
, then, since the curvature is
o(r^{-2})
at infinity (it is actually in
O(r^{-6})
), it is asymptotic to a harmonic tensor
h_{0}
on the asymptotic flat model
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
. It turns out that there are no critical weights for
\Delta_{L,g_{b}}
between
0
and
4

[
Biq13
, Proposition 2.1]
.
∎

11.3.
Infinitesimal deformations of hyperkähler ALE metrics

Let us briefly describe the space of infinitesimal Ricci-flat ALE deformations of hyperkähler ALE metrics. As explained in Proposition
11.2
, they can all be written as a linear combination of:

\mathbf{o}_{ij}=\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+},

where
\omega_{i}^{+}=\omega_{i}^{+}(g_{b})
are the three hyperkähler forms of the manifold asymptotic to the
2
-forms
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})=rdr\wedge\alpha_{i}+r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}
on the Euclidean end (we drop the tilde for this section), and the
\Omega_{j}^{-}
form a basis of the
L^{2}
-cohomology of the manifold, that is the space of closed
L^{2}
anti-selfdual
2
-forms. In the next few sections, we give a somewhat geometric description of a basis of
\Omega_{j}^{-}
of the
L^{2}
-cohomology.

11.3.1.
Kronheimer’s period map and homology

On a
4
-manifold
N
given by the diffeomorphic structure of the minimal resolution of
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\approx\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
, consider
H^{2}_{c}(N)
the cohomology group with compact support, and
k
its dimension. Kronheimer’s Torelli theorem
[
Kro89b
]
provides a bijection between hyperkähler ALE metrics and elements of
H^{2}_{c}(N)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{3}\approx(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{k}
, up to the action of Diff
0
and of
\operatorname{SO}(3)
(corresponding to the various bases of orthogonal triples of Kähler forms with norm
\sqrt{2}
). We will denote our parameter by
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{k})\in(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{k}
which determines a unique hyperkähler metric (up to the action of
\operatorname{SO}(3)
). These coefficients
\zeta
can be chosen to correspond to
k
codimension
2
spheres
(\Sigma_{j})_{j\in\{1,...,k\}}
generating the homology of the manifold in the following manner:

\zeta_{j}=\Big(\int_{\Sigma_{j}}\omega_{1}^{+},\;\int_{\Sigma_{j}}\omega_{2}^{+},\;\int_{\Sigma_{j}}\omega_{3}^{+}\Big)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Remark 11.4
.

Up to a factor
4\pi
present for simplicity, this corresponds to the
\zeta
parameter we have used on an Eguchi-Hanson metric at the beginning of Section
11.1
.

Remark 11.5
.

As in the case of an Eguchi-Hanson metric, the action of
(\varepsilon,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\operatorname{SO}(3)
corresponding to
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{k})\mapsto\varepsilon^{2}(\phi^{*}\zeta_{1},...,\phi^{*}\zeta_{k})=:(\varepsilon,\phi)^{*}\zeta
corresponds to a homothety: there exists a diffeomorphism
s_{(\varepsilon,\phi)}
so that as in (
2.14
), one has:

(11.1)

g_{(\varepsilon,\phi)^{*}\zeta}=\varepsilon^{2}(s_{(\varepsilon,\phi)})_{*}g_{\zeta}.

The intersection of these
2
-spheres follows the Cartan matrix associated to the Dynkin diagram of the singularity. Denote
a_{ij}
the intersection number of
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
and let
A=(a_{ij})_{ij}
. For instance, the matrix whose
i,j
component is the intersection of
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
for a singularity
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{Z}_{5}
, the corresponding matrix is:

A=\begin{bmatrix}-2&1&0&0\\
1&-2&1&0\\
0&1&-2&1\\
0&0&1&-2\end{bmatrix}.

11.3.2.
From homology to cohomology

A common procedure that lets one construct the elements
\Omega_{j}^{-}
of the cohomology is as follows. Consider first
\gamma_{j}
, a representative of the Thom class of the normal bundle of
\Sigma_{j}
supported on the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\Sigma_{j}
for
\varepsilon>0
. It is a closed
2
-form that is supported on a compact arbitrarily close to
\Sigma_{j}
.

Since
H^{1}_{c}(N)=\{0\}
, by standard weighted elliptic theory (or a specific case of the general results of
[
HHM04
]
) there exists a unique
1
-form
\eta_{j}
solving
d^{*}d\eta_{j}=-d^{*}\gamma_{j}
decaying at infinity in
r^{-3}
, see also Proposition
A.9
. We finally define:

\Omega_{j}^{-}:=\gamma_{j}+d\eta_{j}.

An integration by parts and the intersection of the
\Sigma_{i}
lets us compute the
L^{2}
-products of our
\Omega_{i}^{-}
:

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle\Omega_{i}^{-},\Omega_{j}^{-}\rangle_{g_{\zeta}}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\Omega_{i}^{-}\wedge\Omega_{j}^{-}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}(\gamma_{i}+d\eta_{i})\wedge(\gamma_{j}+d\eta_{j})

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\gamma_{i}\wedge\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle=-a_{ij}.

Note that this is also equal to
-\int_{\Sigma_{i}}\Omega_{j}^{-}
. In particular, we find
\int_{M}|\Omega_{i}^{-}|_{g_{\zeta}}^{2}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}=-a_{ii}=2
.

We denote
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{L^{2}}(g_{b})
the space of harmonic anti-selfdual
2
-forms (with respect to
g_{b}
) spanned by the
\Omega_{j}^{-}
.

11.3.3.
L^{2}
-projections on the infinitesimal deformations

From the previous remarks and constructions, we define
\mathbf{o}_{ij}:=\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
which form a basis of the infinitesimal Ricci-flat deformation of hyperkähler ALE metrics. They are traceless, divergence free
2
-tensors decaying at least as
O(r^{-4})
at infinity.

From the previous discussions, we moreover have:

(11.2)

\int_{N}\langle\mathbf{o}_{ij},\mathbf{o}_{kl}\rangle_{g_{\zeta}}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}=-2\delta_{ik}a_{jl},

and in particular
\int_{N}|\mathbf{o}_{ij}|_{g_{\zeta}}^{2}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}=4
in the present conventions.

Let us now consider
h
a
2
-tensor and express its
L^{2}
-projection on the space of infinitesimal deformations in terms of the
L^{2}
-products

H_{ij}=\int_{N}\langle\mathbf{o}_{ij},h\rangle_{g_{\zeta}}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}.
Denoting
A^{-1}=(b_{ij})_{ij}
the inverse of
A
, and
\pi_{\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})}h
the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection of
h
on
\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
, from (
11.2
) we have:

(11.3)

\pi_{\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})}h=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ijk}b_{jk}H_{ik}\mathbf{o}_{ij}.

11.3.4.
Expansion at infinity of the infinitesimal deformations and obstructions

In order to compute the first term at infinity of the above infinitesimal Ricci-flat ALE deformations. We deduce this term thanks to an integration by parts as follows.

Since
\Lambda^{+}
and
\Lambda^{-}
are
pointwise
orthogonal, we have:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{N}\Omega_{j}^{-}\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}

\displaystyle=\int_{N}(\gamma_{j}+d\eta_{j})\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}

(11.4)

\displaystyle=\int_{N}\gamma_{j}\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}+\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{\{r=s\}}\eta_{j}\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}.

Now, since by choosing
\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small,
\gamma_{j}
is arbitrarily concentrated at
\Sigma_{j}
, the first integral
\int_{N}\gamma_{j}\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}
is arbitrarily close to the integral of
\omega_{i}^{+}
on
\Sigma_{j}
. This is exactly the
i
-th coordinate of
\zeta_{j}
denoted
\zeta_{j}^{i}
. Since varying this
\varepsilon
does not affect the second integral,
\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{\{r=s\}}\eta_{j}\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}
, we always have
\int_{N}\gamma_{j}\wedge\omega_{i}^{+}=\zeta_{j}^{i}
.

On the other hand, we know that
\eta_{j}
is harmonic in a neighborhood of infinity so by
[
BH23
]
its leading term in
r^{-3}
must be of the form

\frac{\langle L(x),\cdot\rangle}{r^{4}}
, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
|x|=r
for
L
a
4\times 4
matrix. Since
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})=rdr\wedge\alpha_{i}+r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}
for
(i,j,k)
cyclic permutation of
(1,2,3)
is anti-symmetric, we see that the symmetric part of
L
will not contribute to the boundary term. The only part that will contribute is that proportional to
L=\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})
for which, since
\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})(x)=\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})(r\partial_{r})=r^{2}\alpha_{i}
we find

\int_{\{r=s\}}\frac{\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})(x)}{r^{4}}\wedge r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}=\int_{\{r=s\}}\frac{r^{2}\alpha_{i}}{r^{4}}\wedge r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}=\int_{\{r=s\}}\alpha_{i}\wedge\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}=\frac{\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{3})}{|\Gamma|}.

Since by construction
d\alpha_{i}=2\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}
, we find the associated
2
-form:

d\Big(\frac{\alpha_{i}}{r^{2}}\Big)=-2\frac{rdr\wedge\alpha_{i}-r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}}{r^{4}}=-2\frac{\rho(\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e}))}{r^{4}},

where
\rho(\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e}))=rdr\wedge\alpha_{i}-r^{2}\alpha_{j}\wedge\alpha_{k}

By (
11.4
), we therefore find

\eta_{j}=-\frac{|\Gamma|}{\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{3})}\sum_{i}\zeta_{j}^{i}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{r^{2}}+O(r^{-4})=-\frac{|\Gamma|}{\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{3})}\frac{\zeta_{j}^{+}(\partial_{r},.)}{r^{3}}+O(r^{-4}).

The
r^{-4}
terms of
\Omega_{j}^{-}
are consequently exactly:

\Omega_{j}^{-}=d\eta_{j}=2\frac{|\Gamma|}{\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{3})}\frac{\rho(\zeta_{j}^{+})}{r^{4}}+O(r^{-5})\text{ in a neighborhood of infinity}.

The
r^{-4}
terms of the infinitesimal deformation
\mathbf{o}_{ij}=\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
are:

(11.5)

\mathbf{o}_{ij}=2\frac{|\Gamma|}{\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{3})}\frac{\rho(\zeta_{j}^{+})\circ\omega_{i}^{+}(\mathbf{e})}{r^{4}}+O(r^{-5}).

This is reminiscent of (
2.16
) in the case of an Eguchi-Hanson metric.

11.3.5.
From obstructions to linearized Ricci flows

Extending quadratic terms.

Let
H_{2}
be a quadratic
2
-tensor on
(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma,\mathbf{e})
in Bianchi gauge with respect to the flat metric
\mathbf{e}
and denote
\mathbf{R}^{+}_{p}=\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{e}}^{+,1}(H_{2})
the linearization of the selfdual curvature at the Euclidean metric in the direction
H_{2}
.

We finally reach an extension of Proposition
2.13
to any hyperkähler ALE metric.

Proposition 11.6
.

Up to the addition of an element of
\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
, there exists a unique solution
h_{2,\zeta}
to:

\begin{split}d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2,\zeta})-\Lambda g_{\zeta}&\in\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta}),\\
B_{g_{\zeta}}(h_{2,\zeta})&=0,\\
h_{2,\zeta}-\chi H_{2}&\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta-2},\quad\text{ for all $\beta>0$,}\end{split}

where
\chi
is a cut-off function supported in a neighborhood of infinity,
C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta-2}
is defined in Example
A.6
, and where one has more precisely

(11.6)

d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2,\zeta})-\Lambda g_{\zeta}=-d_{\zeta}g(\mathbf{R}_{p}^{+}(\zeta))\in\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta}).

where
\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{k})=(\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{1}),...,\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{k}))
.

In particular, one has
|\nabla^{g_{\zeta},\,k}h_{2,\zeta}|_{g_{\zeta}}=O(r_{o}^{2-k})
for all
k\geq 0
.

Remark 11.7
.

Exactly like for the Eguchi-Hanson metric, the evolution along Ricci flow is approximately:

\dot{\zeta}=2\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta).

Proof.

Decompose the traceless part
\mathring{h}_{2}
of
h_{2}
as:
\mathring{h}_{2}=\sum_{i}\phi_{i}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
.

Slightly extending the computations of
[
Biq16
]
, we have thanks to
[
BR09
, Formula (4.6)]
the following values for the
L^{2}
-products:

(11.7)

\begin{split}\int_{N}\Big\langle d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2}),\mathbf{o}_{ij}\Big\rangle_{g_{\zeta}}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}&=-|\omega_{i}^{+}|^{2}_{g_{\zeta}}\int_{N}d_{-}d_{-}^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge\Omega_{j}^{-}\\
&=-2\int_{N}dd^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge\Omega_{j}^{-}\\
&=-2\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{\{r=s\}}d^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge\Omega_{j}^{-}\\
&=-2\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{\{r=s\}}dd^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge\eta_{j}^{-}\\
&=-2\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{\{r=s\}}\mathbf{R}^{+}(\omega_{i}^{+})\wedge\eta_{j}^{-}\\
&=-\frac{2|\Gamma|}{\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits(\mathbb{S}^{3})}\sum_{k}\zeta_{j}^{k}\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{\{r=s\}}\mathbf{R}^{+}(\omega_{i}^{+})\wedge\frac{\alpha_{k}}{r^{2}}\\
&=-\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\omega_{i}^{+}),\zeta_{j}^{+}\right\rangle=-\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{j}^{+}),\omega_{i}^{+}\right\rangle.\end{split}

We deduce from (
11.3
) that one has the following
L^{2}(g_{\zeta})
-projection of
d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\chi H_{2})
on
\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
:

(11.8)

\begin{split}\pi_{\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})}\left(d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2})\right)&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ijk}b_{jk}\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{k}^{+}),\omega_{i}^{+}\right\rangle\mathbf{o}_{ij}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ijk}b_{jk}\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{k}^{+}),\omega_{i}^{+}\right\rangle\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}.\end{split}

Let us finally compute the variation of
\zeta
associated to the infinitesimal variation (
11.8
). For this, recall that:

\zeta_{k}^{l}=\int_{\Sigma_{k}}\omega_{l}^{+}.

From
[
Biq16
, Section 2]
or
[
NO24
]
for instance, the variation of
\omega_{l}^{+}
associated to the infinitesimal variation
\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
is
-\delta_{il}\Omega_{j}^{-}
. We deduce that the variation of
\zeta_{k}^{l}
in the direction
\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
is:

-\delta_{il}\int_{\Sigma_{k}}\Omega_{j}^{-}=-\delta_{il}a_{jk}.

The deformation
\pi_{\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})}\left(d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2})\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ijk}b_{jk}\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{k}^{+}),\omega_{i}^{+}\rangle\Omega_{j}^{-}\circ\omega_{i}^{+}
therefore corresponds to the following variation of
\zeta_{m}^{n}
:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ijk}b_{jk}\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{k}^{+}),\omega_{i}^{+}\rangle\delta_{in}a_{jm}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ik}\delta_{km}\delta_{in}\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{k}^{+}),\omega_{i}^{+}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\zeta_{m}^{+}),\omega_{n}^{+}\rangle

Putting these together proves the result since
|\omega_{i}^{+}|^{2}=2
.
∎

Remark 11.8
.

Another way to reach the same conclusion as that of the computation (
11.7
) in the proof of Proposition
11.6

consists in focusing on what happens at the
2
-spheres
\Sigma_{j}
rather than at infinity. Recalling that
\Omega_{j}^{-}=(\gamma_{j}+d\eta_{j})
, we compute on the basis of
[
BR09
, Formula (4.6)]
,

\displaystyle\int_{N}\Big\langle d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2}),\mathbf{o}_{ij}\Big\rangle_{g_{\zeta}}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}

\displaystyle=-2\int_{N}d_{-}d^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge(\gamma_{j}+d\eta_{j})=-2\int_{N}d_{-}d^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge\gamma_{j}

by integration by parts since
d_{-}d_{-}^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}_{L^{2}}(g_{\zeta})
is a decaying closed
2
-form. Now, since
\gamma_{j}
is compactly supported and closed, one has
\int_{N}d_{-}d^{*}\phi_{i}\wedge\gamma_{j}+\int_{N}d_{+}d^{*}\phi_{i}\wedge\gamma_{j}=\int_{N}dd^{*}\phi_{i}\wedge\gamma_{j}=\int_{N}d(d^{*}\phi_{i}\wedge\gamma_{j})=0
. Consequently, we compute

\displaystyle\int_{N}\Big\langle d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2}),\mathbf{o}_{ij}\Big\rangle_{g_{\zeta}}\,d\mu_{g_{\zeta}}

\displaystyle=2\int_{N}d_{+}d^{*}\phi_{i}^{-}\wedge\gamma_{j}=-2\int_{N}\mathbf{R}^{+}(\omega_{i}^{+})\wedge\gamma_{j}=-\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{+}(\omega_{i}^{+}),\zeta_{j}^{+}\right\rangle.

Remark 11.9
.

All of the computations of obstructions to gluing hyperkähler ALE metrics to Einstein orbifolds extend to the case of Ricci soliton orbifolds.

Extending cubic terms.

Now, for some other groups than
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, instead of a development
g_{o}=\mathbf{e}+H_{2}+O(r^{4})
, one has coordinates in which

(11.9)

g_{o}=\mathbf{e}+H_{2}+H_{3}+O(r_{o}^{4}),

for
H_{3}
some cubic harmonic
2
-tensor satisfying
d_{\mathbf{e}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(H_{3})=0
,
B_{\mathbf{e}}H_{3}=0
. This damages the estimates of the first sections of the article. Fortunately, we may also extend the cubic terms
H_{3}
to the orbifold like it was done in Proposition
11.6
.

Proposition 11.10
.

Up to the addition of an element of
\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
, there exists a unique solution
h_{3,\zeta}
to:

\begin{split}d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{3,\zeta})&\in\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta}),\\
B_{g_{\zeta}}(h_{3,\zeta})&=0,\\
h_{3,\zeta}-\chi H_{3}&\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta-1},\quad\text{ for all $\beta>0$,}\end{split}

where
\chi
is a cut-off function supported in a neighborhood of infinity,
C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta-1}
is defined in Example
A.6
, and where one has more precisely

(11.10)

d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{3,\zeta})=O(\varepsilon^{5}\rho^{-4})\in\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta}).

In particular, one has
|\nabla^{g_{\zeta},\,k}h_{3,\zeta}|_{g_{\zeta}}=O(\rho^{3-k})
for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

Consider
\chi
a cut-off function supported at infinity of
(N,g_{\zeta})
. One automatically gets
d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\chi H_{3})=O(r_{b}^{-3})
on
N
since
{g_{\zeta}}-\mathbf{e}=O(r_{b}^{-4})
. From
[
Ozu22b
, Lemma 4.3]
, it is always possible to find
h^{\prime}
such that
d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h^{\prime})+d_{g_{\zeta}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(\chi H_{3})\in\mathbf{O}(g_{\zeta})
.

Next, we estimate the obstruction by integration by parts of
\Delta_{L,g_{\zeta}}(\chi H_{3})
against any element
\mathbf{o}_{ij}=O^{4}+O^{5}+O(\varepsilon^{6}r^{-6})
, with
O^{l}=O(\varepsilon^{l}r^{-l})
. The first boundary term is proportional to
\lim_{r\to\infty}r\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle H_{3},O^{4}\rangle_{g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}}dv_{g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}}
, but the coefficients of
H_{3}
are harmonic in
r^{3}
while the coefficients of
O^{4}
are harmonic in
r^{-4}
, hence their restrictions on
\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma
belong to different eigenspaces of the spherical Laplacian and therefore are orthogonal:

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle H_{3},O^{4}\rangle_{g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}}dv_{g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}}=0.

The next term then comes from the
r^{-5}
term of
\mathbf{o}_{ij}
, up to a multiplicative constant independent on
\zeta
, this is

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle H_{3},O^{5}\rangle_{g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}}dv_{g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}}=O(\varepsilon^{5}).

∎

11.4.
Adapting the proofs of the main theorems to the case of general hyperkähler ALE

The proofs of the main Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
have only been given for the case of Eguchi-Hanson in the first 9 sections of the article. We now explain how the result of the present section together with minor modifications yield the same result for any other hyperkähler ALE spaces.

The main difficulty was to extend the obstruction computations
2.13
to the case of the hyperkähler ALE metrics. This was obtained in
11.6
. The rest of the modifications are minor ones, and in fact most of the proofs are written with a generic metric
g_{\zeta}
instead of
\mathbf{eh}_{\zeta}
for this reason.

•

The estimates of the orbifold metric might be slightly worse if the singularity is
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
, it can be as bad as
O(r^{3})
compared to the
O(r^{4})
when
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, see Remark
3.1
. It requires extending the cubic terms of the orbifold metric to the ALE metric using Proposition
11.10
. Since this is a lower order alteration of the metric in the ALE region alone, and it only involves lower order obstructions, this does not affect the analysis of any section.

•

The most natural basis of
\ker_{L^{2}}\Delta_{L,g_{\zeta}}
is not orthonormal, and has to be orthonormalized in order to run the arguments using the Gram matrix, see Remarks
4.3
and
9.3
. This can of course always be done by Gram-Schmidt procedure.

•

Another potential difficulty is that the metrics obtained for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{3k}\backslash\{0\}
through Kronheimer’s period map are not all
manifolds
: a forbidden set
\mathcal{D}
has to be avoided for the estimates to hold, see Remark
6.4
. This is fortunately the case in our present applications, see Remark
8.2
.

11.5.
Linear (in)stability with respect to other Ricci-flat ALE spaces

A major open question is that of the classification of Ricci-flat ALE metrics. The only known examples are obtained from hyperkähler metrics and are all treated along the present article, but there might exist other Ricci-flat ALE metrics.

In this section, we discuss our construction and stability condition applied to general Ricci-flat ALE metrics: spherical orbifolds are still orbifold point
unstable
, and hyperbolic orbifolds are still orbifold point
stable
. This, of course, recovers the case of hyperkähler ALE metrics.

Note 11.11
.

In this section, we do not attempt to compute the exact constants in our evolutions. We are simply interested in the signs of the derivatives of meaningful quantities.

11.5.1.
General properties of Ricci-flat ALE metrics

We will assume that we are given a Ricci-flat ALE metric
(N,g_{b})
asymptotic to
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
for some finite group
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{O}(4)
acting freely on
\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\{0\}
, we do not assume anything else in this section only.

Infinitesimal Ricci-flat ALE deformations.

Let us denote
\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
the space of traceless and divergence-free infinitesimal Ricci-flat deformations decaying at infinity, they automatically decay in
r^{-4}
at infinity.

In this space
\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
, there is a special space consisting of deformations obtained by homotheties of the whole Ricci-flat ALE metric. They are linear combinations of tensors of the form:

(1)

\mathcal{L}_{Y}g_{b}
, for
Y
a harmonic but not Killing vector field asymptotic to a Killing vector field of
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
or,

(2)

\mathbf{o}_{1}:=(\mathop{\rm Hess}\nolimits_{g_{b}}u)^{\circ}
for
u
the unique solution of
\Delta_{g_{b}}u=4
,
u=\frac{r_{b}^{2}}{2}+O(r_{b}^{-2})
at infinity. Note that this infinitesimal deformation is
nontrivial
on any non-flat Ricci-flat ALE metric. It also moreover always decays exactly at the slowest
r^{-4}
rate.

Note that the Killing vector fields of
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
are
\Gamma
-invariant linear combinations of the Killing vector fields
\omega_{i}^{\pm}(\mathbf{e})(r\partial_{r})
fixing zero on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

In this section, we focus on the effect of the deformation
\mathbf{o}_{1}
compared to the rest, and consider the splitting:

(11.11)

\mathbf{O}(g_{b})=:\mathbb{R}\mathbf{o}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\perp}}{{\oplus}}\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})\text{ in $L^{2}(g_{b})$}.

We will justify the interpretation of
\mathbf{o}_{1}
as a “rescaling” deformation below.

Gauges at infinity and renormalized volume.

In
[
BH23
]
, a specific gauge is introduced for Ricci-flat ALE metrics. It is based on a Constant Mean Curvature foliation of infinity, from which Biquard and Hein obtain specific ALE coordinates as in Definition
2.4
,
\Phi_{0}:N\backslash K\to\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma\backslash B_{\mathbf{e}}(R)/\Gamma
in which one has:

(11.12)

\Phi_{0}^{*}g_{b}=\mathbf{e}+H^{4}+O(r^{-5}),

and
H^{4}
is a harmonic symmetric
2
-tensor satisfying
H^{4}(\partial_{r},\cdot)=0
,
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}H^{4}=0
and
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}H^{4}=0
. We will call any such ALE coordinates
strongly radial
.

A measure of the “size” of the ALE metric, or of its distance from its flat asymptotic cone
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
is the
renormalized volume
computed as follows: denote
U_{r}
the interior of the hypersurface
\partial U_{r}=\Phi_{0}^{-1}(r\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma)
,

(11.13)

\mathcal{V}(g_{b}):=\lim_{r\to\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{g_{b}}U_{r}-\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}B_{\mathbf{e}}(r)/|\Gamma|\right).

This quantity is nonpositive and vanishes
only
if
g_{b}
is flat. It is interpreted as the “size” of the Ricci-flat ALE metric
g_{b}
, it is a geometric quantity, independent on a choice of coordinates and scales like a volume, as follows: for
s>0
,

(11.14)

\mathcal{V}(s^{2}g_{b})=s^{4}\mathcal{V}(g_{b}).

In order to simplify later proofs, let us first note that the renormalized volume can be computed in more general coordinates than the above strictly radial gauge, and that it does not require the manifold to be Ricci-flat, but simply to have its Ricci curvature decaying fast enough to yield an expansion as in (
11.12
).

Lemma 11.12
.

Let
(N,g)
be an ALE manifold, and assume that there exist ALE coordinates
\Phi_{1}
so that

(11.15)

\Phi_{1}^{*}g=\mathbf{e}+H^{4}+O(r^{-5}),

and
H^{4}
is a harmonic symmetric
2
-tensor satisfying
H^{4}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=0
(only!),
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}H^{4}=0
and
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}H^{4}=0
. We call such coordinates
weakly radial
.

Then, the renormalized volume of
g
is also computed as follows from
U^{1}_{r}
the interior of
\Phi_{1}^{-1}(r\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma)
,

\mathcal{V}(g)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{g}U^{1}_{r}-\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}B_{\mathbf{e}}(r)/|\Gamma|\right),

and
\mathcal{V}(g)
is independent of the choice of diffeomorphism
\Phi_{1}
with the above properties.

Proof.

With the exact same proof as in
[
BH23
]
, even if
g
is not Ricci-flat, we obtain the existence of a CMC foliation and that of a diffeomorphism
\Phi_{0}
for which

(11.16)

\Phi_{0}^{*}g=\mathbf{e}+H^{4}+O(r^{-5}),

and
H^{4}
is a harmonic symmetric
2
-tensor satisfying
H^{4}(\partial_{r},\cdot)=0
,
\mathop{\rm tr}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}H^{4}=0
and
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}H^{4}=0
. The existence of the limit defining
\mathcal{V}(g)
also follows from
[
BH23
]
.

In
[
BH23
]
, the renormalized volume is not computed from a CMC foliation, this is simply a way to make the value canonical. Instead, its value is computed in some strictly radial system of coordinates. All such coordinate systems lead to hypersurfaces
\Phi_{0}^{-1}(r\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma)
which are close to the actual CMC foliation at a fast enough rate
r^{-5}
, see
[
BH23
]
. This ensures that the value of the renormalized volume is independent of the
strongly radial
system of coordinates chosen.

What remains to be proved is that if one instead has ALE coordinates from
\Phi_{1}
with only
H^{4}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=0
instead of
H^{4}(\partial_{r},\cdot)=0
, then the value of
\mathcal{V}(g)
is unchanged. The idea is that from
[
BH23
, (2.21)]
one may go from
\Phi_{1}
to some strongly radial system of coordinates
\Phi_{0}
through a diffeomorphism corresponding to following the flow of
X_{2}:=\Phi_{1}^{-1}(\frac{L_{2}x}{r^{4}})
for
L_{2}
a traceless symmetric matrix. Let us denote
(\Phi_{t})_{t\in(0,1)}
the associated family of diffeomorphisms,
\Sigma^{t}_{r}:=\Phi_{t}^{-1}(r)
, and
U^{t}_{r}\subset N
the interior of
\Sigma^{t}_{r}
.

Then, the variation evolution of the volume of
U^{t}_{r}
is computed as follows:

\frac{d}{dt}\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{g_{b}}U^{t}_{r}=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\int_{\Sigma^{t}_{r}}\langle X_{2},\nu_{\Sigma^{t}_{r}}\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\sigma_{\Sigma^{t}_{r}}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle L_{2}x,x\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}+\gamma(r,t),

where
\nu_{\Sigma^{t}_{r}}=\frac{x}{|x|}+O(r^{-4})
is the exterior
g_{b}
-normal to
\Sigma^{t}_{r}
, and where
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\gamma(r,t)\to 0
as
r\to+\infty
. Now, since
L_{2}
is traceless, the leading term
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle L_{2}x,x\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}
vanishes, and the value of the limit

\lim_{r\to+\infty}\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{g_{b}}U^{t}_{r}-\mathop{\rm Vol}\nolimits_{\mathbf{e}}B_{\mathbf{e}}(r)/|\Gamma|

is independent of
t
.
∎

In such weakly radial coordinates produced by Lemma
11.12
, one may also intriguingly compute the renormalized volume from the expansion of
u
, the unique solution to
\Delta_{g_{b}}u=4
,
u=\frac{r^{2}}{2}+o(1)
. In
[
BH23
]
, the following result is only stated for Ricci-flat ALE metrics, but extends verbatim to the setting of Lemma
11.12
:

Proposition 11.13

(
[
BH23
]
)
.

Under the assumptions, and in the gauge of Lemma
11.12
, the above function
u
has the following development:

(11.17)

\Phi_{1}^{*}u=\frac{r^{2}}{2}-\frac{2|\Gamma|}{|\mathbb{S}^{3}|}\frac{\mathcal{V}(g)}{r^{2}}+o(r^{-2}).

In particular, the value of the
r^{-2}
-term in the expansion of
u
determines completely the renormalized volume in the coordinates given by
\Phi_{1}
.

11.5.2.
Variations of the renormalized volume

Let us explain why the deformation
\mathbf{o}_{1}
is interpreted as a “rescaling” and detects the variations of the renormalized volume.

Let
\mathbf{o}\in\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
be an infinitesimal Ricci-flat ALE deformation of
g_{b}
. We will compute the renormalized volume of
g_{t}:=g_{b}+t\mathbf{o}
at first order, and we will denote with a “dot” the derivatives with respect to
t
at
t=0
of various tensors. Our goal is to prove the following statement.

Proposition 11.14
.

If
g_{t}=g_{b}+t\mathbf{o}_{1}
, then one has:

(11.18)

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(g_{t})=2\mathcal{V}(g_{b})\text{ at $t=0$}.

On the other hand, let
\mathbf{o}_{0}\in\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
and if
g_{t}=g_{b}+t\mathbf{o}_{0}
, then, one has

(11.19)

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(g_{t})=0\text{ at $t=0$}.

In other words,
\mathbf{o}_{1}
is the only element of
\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
that can vary the renormalized volume of a Ricci-flat ALE metric.

Let us first prove the simpler (
11.18
). The deformation
\mathbf{o}_{1}=g_{b}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\nabla^{g_{b}}u_{b}}g_{b}
infinitesimally corresponds to the commuting composition of the action by the diffeomorphism generated by the vector field
-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{g_{b}}u_{b}
and the rescaling
(1+t)g_{b}
. The diffeomorphism does not affect the value of the renormalized volume while the rescaling
(1+t)g_{b}
modifies the value of the reduced volume according to the rescaling property (
11.14
), hence the formula:

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}(g_{t})=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{V}((1+t)g_{b})=2\mathcal{V}(g_{b}).

We now deal with the more subtle case of (
11.19
) through a series of partial results.

Lemma 11.15
.

Denote
u_{t}
the unique solution of
\Delta_{g_{t}}u_{t}=4
,
u_{t}=\frac{r_{b}^{2}}{2}+o(1)
in the same ALE coordinates as those of
g_{b}
. Then we find the following development:

\dot{u}=\frac{a}{r^{2}}+o(r^{-2}),

where

a=-\frac{|\Gamma|}{2|\mathbb{S}^{3}|}\int_{N}\langle\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}_{1}\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\mu_{g_{b}}.

Proof.

Let us take a look at the equation satisfied by
\dot{u}
. Using the fact that
\mathbf{o}
is traceless and divergence-free, the (classical) formula for the variation of a Laplacian helps us find that:

(11.20)

\Delta_{g_{b}}\dot{u}=\langle\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}_{1}\rangle_{g_{b}},

and there is a unique such solution decaying like
r^{-2+\varepsilon}
at infinity for
\varepsilon>0
by standard elliptic theory in weighted Hölder spaces. It actually decays like
r^{-2}
: its leading term is a multiple of the Green function
r^{-2}
of
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\Gamma
because of the decay of the righthand-side. Let us compute this leading coefficient
a\in\mathbb{R}
of
\dot{u}=\frac{a}{r^{2}}+O(r^{-2.1})
.

In particular, integrating the equation (
11.20
) by parts over the whole manifold we find:

\displaystyle-2a|\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma|=\lim_{s\to+\infty}\int_{\{r_{b}=s\}}\partial_{r_{b}}\dot{u}\,d\sigma_{S_{g_{b}}(p,s)}=\int_{N}\langle\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}_{1}\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\mu_{g_{b}}.

∎

In particular, we notice that if
\mathbf{o}
is
L^{2}(g_{b})
-orthogonal to
\mathbf{o}_{1}
, then
a=0
. This is however not enough to ensure that the first variation of the renormalized volume vanishes from Proposition
11.13
: we still need to ensure that the ALE coordinates given by
\Phi
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
11.12
for the metrics
g_{t}
, in other words, we need to verify that
\mathbf{o}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=0
for any
\mathbf{o}\in\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
.

Lemma 11.16
.

Let
\mathbf{o}\in\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
, then
\mathbf{o}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=o(r^{-4})
. On the other hand, one has

(11.21)

\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{4}\mathbf{o}_{1}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=\frac{|\Gamma|}{|\mathbb{S}^{3}|}\|\mathbf{o}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}^{2}.

Proof.

According to the decomposition of harmonic
2
-tensors that are
O(r^{-4})
at infinity of
[
BH23
, Proposition 2.6]
, we find that for a constant
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbf{o}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=\frac{a^{\prime}}{r^{4}}+o(r^{-4})
, and our goal is to show that
a^{\prime}=0
.

Let us write
\mathbf{o}_{1}:=(\mathcal{L}_{X}g_{b})^{\circ}
for
X=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{g_{b}}u
. Applying the formula
[
Ozu24
, (21)]
to
T=\mathbf{o}
, and
X
as above, one obtains

(11.22)

\int_{\partial U_{r}}\mathbf{o}(X,\nu_{r})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{U_{r}}\langle\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}_{1}\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\mu_{g_{b}}.

Now, taking a limit of (
11.22
) as
r\to+\infty
, one finds

(11.23)

\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{3}|}{|\Gamma|}a^{\prime}=\int_{N}\langle\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}_{1}\rangle_{g_{b}}\,d\mu_{g_{b}},

and consequently, if
\mathbf{o}\in\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
,
a^{\prime}=0
as announced, and we similarly obtain the formula for
\mathbf{o}_{1}
.
∎

This concludes the proof of (
11.19
).

Moduli space of Ricci-flat modulo obstructions ALE metrics

As constructed in
[
Ozu24
, Appendix B]
, close to any Ricci-flat ALE metric, there is a real-analytic set of metrics
g_{b,v}
parametrized by
v
in the unit ball of the vector space
\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
endowed with the
L^{2}(g_{b})
norm.

Definition 11.17
.

The metrics
g_{b,v}
are uniquely determined as satisfying:

(1)

\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g_{b,v})\in\pi_{\ker B_{g_{b,v}}}\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
, and
\|\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g_{b,v})\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}\leq\|v\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}^{2}
, where
\pi_{\ker B_{g_{b,v}}}
is the projection on the kernel of
B_{g_{b,v}}
as in
[
Biq13
, (45)]
,

(2)

B_{g_{b}}g_{b,v}=0
,

(3)

\mathcal{V}(g_{b,v})=\mathcal{V}(g_{b})
, and
g_{b,v}-g_{b}\perp_{L^{2}(g_{b})}\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
, and

(4)

|\nabla^{g_{b},\,k}(g_{b,v}-g_{b})|_{g_{b}}\leq C_{k}\|v\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}^{2}(1+r)^{-4-k}
for
C_{k}>0
.

There are also deformations of
g_{b}
corresponding to the infinitesimal deformation
\mathbf{o}_{1}
: define

(1)

\partial_{s}g_{b}^{s}=g_{b}^{s}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\nabla^{g_{b}^{s}}u^{s}}g_{b}^{s}=:\mathbf{o}_{1}^{s}
,

(2)

where
u^{s}
is the only solution of
\Delta_{g_{b}^{s}}u^{s}=4
with
u^{s}=\frac{r^{2}}{2}+o(1)
which is also characterized by
\Delta_{g_{b}^{s}}\partial_{s}u^{s}=|g_{b}^{s}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\nabla^{g_{b}^{s}}u^{s}}g_{b}^{s}|^{2}_{g_{b}^{s}}
from Lemma
11.15
.

The metrics
g_{b}^{s}
are all homothetic since
\partial_{s}(e^{-s}g_{b}^{s})=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\nabla^{g_{b}^{s}}u^{s}}(e^{-s}g_{b}^{s})
, hence there exists a diffeomorphism
\phi_{s}
so that
g_{b}^{s}=e^{s}\phi_{s}^{*}g_{b}
. We may then define the metrics
g^{s}_{b,v}=e^{s}\phi_{s}^{*}g_{b,v}
.

11.5.3.
Linear (in)stability of orbifolds with constant curvature

Thanks to the previous section, we can interpret the obstructions against the infinitesimal deformation
\mathbf{o}_{1}
as the way to measure how the scale of a Ricci-flat ALE metric evolves along Ricci flow.
We will only treat the simplest case of an Einstein orbifold with vanishing Weyl curvature at its singular points (such as spherical and hyperbolic orbifolds).

Let
g_{o}
be an Einstein orbifold with
\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g_{o})=\Lambda g_{o}
and vanishing Weyl curvature at a singular point
p
where one has the development:

(11.24)

g_{o}=\mathbf{e}+H_{2}+O_{\mathbf{e}}(r_{o}^{4}),

where
H_{2}
is a quadratic
2
-tensor in Bianchi gauge with respect to the flat metric
\mathbf{e}
, and
r_{o}
is the distance to zero for
\mathbf{e}
.
The following result echoes Propositions
2.13
and
11.6
.

Proposition 11.18
.

Up to the addition of an element of
\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
, there exists a unique solution
h_{2,\zeta}
to:

\begin{split}d_{g_{b}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2,\zeta})-\Lambda g_{b}&\in\mathbf{O}(g_{b}),\\
B_{g_{b}}(h_{2,\zeta})&=0\\
h_{2,\zeta}-\chi H_{2}&\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta-2},\quad\text{ for all $\beta>0$,}\end{split}

where
\chi
is a cut-off function supported in a neighborhood of infinity,
C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta-2}
is defined in Example
A.6
, and where one has more precisely for
c=c(\Gamma)>0
,

(11.25)

d_{g_{b}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2})-\Lambda g_{b}=-c\Lambda\mathbf{o}_{1}.

Proof.

Let us first prove that the obstruction vanishes against an element
\mathbf{o}_{0}\in\mathbf{O}_{0}(g_{b})
. For this, we use the development of a spherical or hyperbolic orbifold satisfying in Bianchi gauge with respect to the flat cone:

(11.26)

g_{o}=\mathbf{e}+H_{2}+O(r_{o}^{4}),

where
H_{2}
is a multiple of
r^{2}(3dr^{2}+2r^{2}g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}})
if
\mathbf{e}=dr^{2}+r^{2}g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}
.

The
2
-tensor
\mathbf{o}_{0}
admits an expansion at infinity: for
O^{4}_{0}
, the homogeneous harmonic leading term of
\mathbf{o}_{0}
in
r^{-4}
,

(11.27)

\mathbf{o}_{0}=O^{4}_{0}+O(r^{-5}).

The obstruction to solving the equation for
h_{2}
against
\mathbf{o}_{0}
from
[
Ozu20
, Proposition 5.3, Remarque 5.5]
is then a multiple of

(11.28)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle O^{4}_{0},H_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}}\,d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}.

Since
O^{4}_{0}
is
\mathbf{e}
-traceless and has a vanishing
(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})
component, it is pointwise orthogonal to the
2
-tensor
H_{2}=r^{2}(3dr^{2}+2r^{2}g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}})
, and the above integral vanishes.

As computed in (
11.21
), we have
O^{4}_{1}(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})=\frac{|\Gamma|}{|\mathbb{S}^{3}|}\frac{\|\mathbf{o}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}^{2}}{r^{4}}
for
\mathbf{o}_{1}=O^{4}_{1}+o(r^{-4})
with leading homogeneous harmonic term
O^{4}_{1}
. Consequently, since
O^{4}_{1}
is traceless, there is a positive numerical constant
c_{2}>0
so that

(11.29)

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}\langle O^{4}_{1},H_{2}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}}\,d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{3}/\Gamma}=c_{2}\Lambda\frac{|\Gamma|}{|\mathbb{S}^{3}|}\|\mathbf{o}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}^{2}.

Consequently, since the projection on
\mathbf{o}_{1}
compensates the coefficient
\|\mathbf{o}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(g_{b})}^{2}
we can solve our equation with explicit obstruction: for
c=c(\Gamma)>0
,

(11.30)

d_{g_{b}}\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(h_{2})-\Lambda g_{b}=-c\Lambda\mathbf{o}_{1}.

∎

Remark 11.19
.

If the orbifold had nonvanishing Weyl curvature at its singular point, we would see additional obstructions couplings of the
\pm
-selfdual Weyl curvature of the orbifold at its singular point, and of the
\mp
-selfdual Weyl curvature of the ALE metric at infinity, see
[
Ozu24
]
.

An approximate Ricci flow.

Notice that
t\mapsto g^{2c\Lambda t}_{b,0}
solves
\partial_{t}g^{2c\Lambda t}_{b,0}=2c\Lambda\mathbf{o}_{1}
. From the equation (
11.25
) we deduce as in Lemma
3.8
that an approximate renormalized Ricci flow is obtained by gluing a metric
g^{2c\Lambda t}_{b,0}
to a spherical or hyperbolic orbifold
(M_{o},g_{o})
.

In particular, the bubbles grow when glued to spherical orbifolds and shrink when glued to hyperbolic orbifolds, at an exponential rate. This is consistent with the case of hyperkähler Ricci-flat ALE metrics. Similar observations hold with orbifold solitons with vanishing Weyl curvature at their singular point, e.g. quotients of Bryant soliton or cylinders
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

11.5.4.
Constructing ancient and immortal Ricci flows from general Ricci-flat ALE metrics

Let us list the properties that we used along the article in order to construct immortal and ancient Ricci flows from hyperkähler ALE bubbles which might not be satisfied by general Ricci-flat ALE metrics.

(1)

Stability
: hyperkähler metrics satisfy Proposition
11.2
.

(2)

Explicit obstruction
: the obstructions can be computed as in Proposition
11.6
.

(3)

Integrability
: for any infinitesimal Ricci-flat ALE deformation
\mathbf{o}
, there is an associated
curve
of Ricci-flat ALE metrics with initial speed
\mathbf{o}
.

(4)

Canonical parametrization
: Kronheimer’s period map gives a natural, analytic parametrization of the moduli space.

We believe that none of these properties specific to the hyperkähler ALE metrics are essential to our construction, except that of
stability
, which ensures that the Ricci-flat ALE metric satisfies an appropriate Liouville theorem.

(1)

Stability
: intuitively, Ricci-flat ALE metrics appearing “generically” along the Ricci flow should be
stable
, so it is reasonable to assume this property. By Remark
5.1
, there is a good chance that it is not necessary.

(2)

Explicit obstruction
: Proposition
11.18
provides explicit obstructions when the orbifold has vanishing Weyl curvature at its singular point.

(3)

Integrability
: even if the smooth moduli space of nearby Ricci-flat ALE metrics is not smooth, there is a smooth moduli space of Ricci-flat ALE modulo obstruction metrics
g^{s}_{b,v}
.

(a)

everything is solved modulo obstructions everywhere along the article until the compensation of obstructions through an unaffected fixed point setup, and

(b)

the obstructions are of very low order with respect to the other infinitesimal deformations, as in the case of hyperkähler ALE metrics. Indeed, the obstruction of Proposition
11.18
only involves
\mathbf{o}_{1}
, and the nonintegrability, only induces quadratic errors in the parameter
v
, which is already negligible on its own.

(4)

Canonical parametrization
: this was not necessary at all, and merely links our evolution equation to parameters used in the literature.

Appendix A
Real-analytic variations of tensors solving equations

In this section, we present a way to precisely analyze the dependence of the metrics and special tensors as the parameter
\zeta
of Kronheimer’s period map varies. This leads to analytic controls of these tensors seen as functions of
\zeta
with specific decays.

Remark A.1
.

In the particular case of the explicit Eguchi-Hanson metric, all of the estimates below can be obtained through explicit but painful computations.

The analytic dependence will be proved by implicit function theorems for analytic maps between Banach spaces.

A.1.
Real-analyticity of operations

We prove that our maps are analytic to start with.

A.1.1.
Real-analytic Mappings Between Banach Spaces

Let us recall some basic definitions and theorems about real-analytic mappings between Banach spaces and real-analytic subsets that will be useful to us.

Let
a_{k}:V^{k}\to W
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
be continuous symmetric
k
-linear forms on
V
with values in
W
. The
power series
denoted
x\in V\mapsto\sum_{k}a_{k}x^{k}\in W
from a Banach space
V
to a Banach space
W
is defined as the sum of
a_{k}(x,\ldots,x)
. We say that it
converges
if the real sum
\sum_{k}\|a_{k}(x,\ldots,x)\|_{W}
converges. From this, we derive the definition of an real-analytic map.

Definition A.2

(Real-analytic maps)
.

Let
V
and
W
be Banach spaces, and
U
an open set in
V
. A map
f:U\to W
is said to be
real-analytic
if for every point
x\in U
,
f
is equal to a convergent power series in a neighborhood of
x
.

This definition is adapted to the usual framework of analysis, and in particular, the implicit function theorem is valid for this regularity.

Lemma A.3

(
[
Whi65
, Implicit Function Theorem, p. 1081]
)
.

Let
X,Y,
and
Z
be Banach spaces, and
A
an open set in
X\times Y
. Suppose a real-analytic map
f:A\to Z
satisfies for
(x_{0},y_{0})\in A
,

(1)

f(x_{0},y_{0})=0,

(2)

d_{(x_{0},y_{0})}f(0,.):Y\to Z
is a homeomorphism.

Then,

(1)

there exists
N(x_{0})
, an open neighborhood of
x_{0}
in
X
, and a unique continuous map
g:N(x_{0})\to Y
such that
g(x_{0})=y_{0}
,
(x,g(x))\in A
for
x\in N(x_{0})
, and

f(x,g(x))=0,

(2)

g
is analytic,

(3)

d_{x}g=-\big(d_{(x,g(x))}f(0,.)\big)^{-1}\circ\big(d_{(x,g(x))}f(.,0)\big)
.

A.1.2.
Real-analytic operations between weighted Hölder spaces

It will be crucial to note that operations between tensors having only a local
H^{s}
regularity for
s
large enough depending on the dimension or having a
C^{k,\alpha}
regularity for
k
larger than the number of derivations are in fact analytic. This may be obtained by the proof of
[
Koi83
, Lemma 13.7]
, but we give a simpler proof in the case of (weighted) Hölder spaces. The main tool is the theory developed in
[
Pal68
]
and the crucial property is that for these regularities, the set of functions equipped with pointwise multiplication forms a Banach algebra.

This leads us to define classes of function spaces well adapted to our analysis. For a tensor
s
, a point
x
,
\alpha>0
and a metric
g
, the pointwise Hölder semi-norm
[s]_{C^{\alpha}(g)}(x)
is defined as

[s]_{C^{\alpha}(g)}(x):=\sup_{y\in B_{g}(x,\,\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits_{g}(x)/2)}\frac{\left|s(x)-(P^{g}_{x,y})^{\ast}s(y)\right|_{g}}{d_{g}(x,y)^{\alpha}},

where
P_{x,y}^{g}
denotes the parallel transport with respect to
g
along the unique minimizing geodesic joining
x
to
y
.

Definition A.4

(Weighted Hölder spaces and algebra)
.

Let
w
be a smooth function called a “weight”, and
\rho
be a positive function called a “scale” on a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, and let
0<\alpha<1
. For any tensor
s
on
M
, we define

\displaystyle\|s\|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{w,\rho}(g)}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{M}w\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}\rho^{i}|\nabla^{g,\,i}s|_{g}+\rho^{k+\alpha}[\nabla^{g,\,k}s]_{C^{\alpha}(g)}\right).

We will call such a function space an
algebra
if
w\geq 1
.

Remark A.5
.

Typically,
\rho
is chosen as equivalent to the injectivity or conjugate radius, while
w
is a function of
\rho
.

Example A.6
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<\alpha<1
and let
(N,g_{b})
be an ALE orbifold. If we define for any tensor
s
on
N
,

\displaystyle\|s\|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{\beta}(g_{b})}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{N}r_{b}^{\beta}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}r_{b}^{i}|\nabla^{g_{b},\,i}s|_{g_{b}}+r_{b}^{k+\alpha}[\nabla^{g_{b},\,k}s]_{C^{\alpha}(g_{b})}\right),

then, this is a weighted Hölder algebra as long as
\beta\geq 0
. Here
r_{b}
denotes some smoothing of the maximum of
1
and the distance from an arbitrary point in
N
defined with respect to
g_{b}
.

Let us now explain which operations are analytic in these weighted Hölder algebras.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}
be tensors on
M
, and associated norms
{C^{k_{1},\alpha_{1}}_{w_{1},\rho}(g)},\dots,{C^{k_{m},\alpha_{m}}_{w_{m},\rho}(g)}
. Then, we have:

(1)

\|s_{1}\|_{C^{k_{1},\alpha_{1}}_{w_{1},\rho}(g)}\leq\|s_{1}\|_{C^{k_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}_{w_{1}^{\prime},\rho}(g)}
for
k_{1}\leq k^{\prime}_{1}
,
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha^{\prime}_{1}
and
w_{1}\leq w^{\prime}_{1}
,

(2)

\|\nabla^{g,\,l}s_{1}\|_{C^{k_{1}-l,\alpha_{1}}_{w_{1},\rho}(g)}\leq\|s_{1}\|_{C^{k_{1},\alpha_{1}}_{\rho^{-l}w_{1},\rho}(g)}
for
l\leq k_{1}
,

(3)

if
w\geq 1
, and if
(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})\mapsto q(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})
is both

•

at each point a
pointwise

m
-linear operation on the tensors
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}
, i.e. a fiberwise
m
-linear form with a control
|q(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})|_{g}<C|s_{1}|_{g}\dots|s_{m}|_{g}
for
C>0
uniform on the manifold,

•

defined through contractions of the tensors
s_{i}
with the metric
g
,

then, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
depending on
q
and the parameters
k_{i},\alpha_{1},w_{i},\rho
and the metric, so that

\|q(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})\|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{w,\rho}}\leq C^{\prime}\|s_{1}\|_{C^{k_{1},\alpha_{1}}_{w_{1},\rho}}\dots\|s_{m}\|_{C^{k_{m},\alpha_{m}}_{w_{m},\rho}},

where
k:=\min(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})
,
\alpha:=\min(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})
,
w:=w_{1}\dots w_{m}
.

(4)

If
w\geq 1
, and if
h
is a
2
-tensor and
\|h\|_{C^{k_{1},\alpha_{1}}_{w_{1},\rho}}<\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
depending only on the dimension, then the map
h\mapsto(g+h)^{-1}
from
(0,2)-
tensors to
(2,0)-
tensors is real analytic as long as
w_{1}>1
.

Proof.

The first two points are obvious from the definitions of the norms, and the third point may be checked pointwise using a uniform bound on
q
and its derivatives. The assumption
w\geq 1
is crucial and is simply used to ensure that
w^{m}\geq w
to control multilinear terms.

The last point is proved through the local formula for the inverse: in a
g
-orthonormal basis, the
i,j
component of
(g+h)^{-1}
is
(g+h)^{ij}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(-h)^{k}_{ij}
, where
(-h)^{k}
is the composition of
k
times of the matrices of
-h
in the chosen basis–which are multilinear operations in the sense of point
3
. Indeed, the composition
k
-times is a continuous multilinear operation
q_{k}
which satisfies for a dimensional constant
C
,
|(-h)^{k}|=|q_{k}(h,\dots,h)|<C^{k}|h|^{k}
pointwise. For
|h|
small enough the series
(g+h)^{ij}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(-h)^{k}_{ij}
is a converging power series in the sense of Banach spaces, Definition
A.2
.
∎

The maps we are interested in only involve various contractions and differentiations and will therefore be analytic as long as we choose the function spaces accordingly.

A.2.
Real-analytic dependence of solutions of parametrized equations

The goal is now to prove the analytic dependence of our metrics and tensors with respect to their parameter
\zeta
. Note that we will not need precise controls over our operators or their radii of convergence. Indeed, up to the action of
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\operatorname{SO}(3)
described in (
11.1
), we will be able to work close to
\zeta
of norm
1
(which fixes the renormalized volume of
[
BH23
]
) away from the forbidden set
\mathcal{D}
of codimension
3
, corresponding to orbifold metrics.

Recall that the linearization of the map
g\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Ric}\nolimits(g)-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{B_{g_{b}}(g)}(g)
at
g_{b}
, denoted by
-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{L,g_{b}}:C^{2,\alpha}_{\beta}\mapsto C^{0,\alpha}_{\beta+2}
is a Fredholm operator for
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,4)
and that its kernels and cokernels are equal to a space denoted by
\mathbf{O}(g_{b})
of symmetric, traceless, divergence-free
2
-tensors decaying like
r^{-4}
,
[
Ozu20
]
. It is therefore possible to find parametrizations in which the metric depends in a real-analytic way (even if they are not hyperkähler). This yields the family of metrics of Definition
11.17
at a general Ricci-flat ALE metric, see
[
Ozu24
, Appendix B]
.

However, we are presently given a specific parametrization through Kronheimer’s period map and the associated parameter
\zeta
. We want to show the analyticity with respect to this specific parameter.

We will only consider hyperkähler spaces classified in
[
Kro89b
]
. They are parametrized by
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{3k}
, for
k
the dimension of the
L^{2}
-cohomology of the ALE space, a topological quantity. By
[
HHM04
]
, for any ALE metric
g
, the space
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{L^{2}}(g)
of
L^{2}
-harmonic
2
-forms is a well-defined
k
